| `central-southern-africa:01` | **Congolese Rumba & Soukous (Sebene Guitar)** | Kinshasa (DR Congo) & Brazzaville (Republic of the Congo) | Mid-20th-century Afro-Cuban reclamation evolving into high-speed, liquid-clean 'sebene' lead guitar arpeggios, snare rimshots, and Lingala harmonies. |
| `central-southern-africa:02` | **Kinshasa Congotronics (Amplified Likembe)** | Kinshasa, Democratic Republic of the Congo | Urban traditional Bazombo/Luba trance music played on homemade electromagnetic-pickup thumb pianos (likembe) through distorting megaphone amps. |
| `central-southern-africa:03` | **Mutuashi** | Kasai Region (Mbuji-Mayi & Kananga), DR Congo | Luba women-led 12/8 dance music combining traditional tshinkimbwa percussion with bouncy electric guitar lines. |
| `central-southern-africa:04` | **Ndombolo** | Kinshasa, DR Congo | 1990s–present kinetic Congolese dance style propelled by fast snare rolls, animated vocal hype-men (atalaku), and interlocking guitars. |
| `central-southern-africa:05` | **Cameroonian Makossa** | Douala, Cameroon | Funky urban coastal dance music anchored by virtuosic melodic bass guitar lines, punchy horn sections, and Duala vocals. |
| `central-southern-africa:06` | **Cameroonian Bikutsi** | Yaoundé (Centre/South Region), Cameroon | Fierce, stomping 6/8 Beti dance music translating wooden mvet zither and balafon rhythms onto muted, percussive electric guitars. |
| `central-southern-africa:07` | **Cameroonian Assiko & Bend-Skin** | Bassa & Bamileke Regions, Cameroon | Acoustic guitar-and-forked-bottle percussion music (assiko) and West Region metallic drum street dance (bend-skin). |
| `central-southern-africa:08` | **Gabonese Bwiti Ngombi Harp & Pierre Akendengué Folk-Afro-Psych** | Libreville & Ngounié, Gabon | Sacred eight-string arched harp (ngombi) polyphony and poetic Central African acoustic/electric art-folk. |
| `central-southern-africa:09` | **Angolan Semba** | Luanda, Angola | Golden-age 1960s–70s urban Angolan dance music driven by the dikanza bamboo scraper, ngoma drum, and weeping solo electric guitar. |
| `central-southern-africa:10` | **Angolan Rebita** | Luanda, Angola | Early-20th-century Afro-Portuguese salon circle-dance music played on concertina accordion, mukindu drum, and dikanza. |
| `central-southern-africa:11` | **Angolan Kuduro** | Luanda (Sambizanga / Rangel), Angola | 1990s ~130–140 BPM electronic street music fusing traditional Angolan kilapanga/semba percussion with European techno synths. |
| `central-southern-africa:12` | **Angolan Kazukuta & Kabetula** | Luanda & Malanje, Angola | Carnival stomp rhythms played on hungu musical bow, cans, whistles, and syncopated guitar. |
| `central-southern-africa:13` | **Zambian Zamrock** | Lusaka & Copperbelt (Kitwe / Ndola), Zambia | 1970s Zambian rock explosion combining fuzzy Jimi Hendrix/Black Sabbath guitar riffs and James Brown funk breaks with Bemba/Nyanja lyrics. |
| `central-southern-africa:14` | **Zambian Kalindula** | Luapula & Lusaka, Zambia | Upbeat dance music named after the giant one-or-two-string bass banjo (kalindula), later played by fast two-guitar electric bands. |
| `central-southern-africa:15` | **Zimbabwean Chimurenga (Mbira Guitar Music)** | Harare, Zimbabwe | Liberation-era style created by Thomas Mapfumo translating sacred Shona 22-key mbira dzavadzimu polyphony onto electric guitars and hosho shakers. |
| `central-southern-africa:16` | **Zimbabwean Jit & Sungura** | Harare & Chitungwiza, Zimbabwe | Fast, bubbling 4/4 snare-and-hi-hat Zimbabwean party guitar music combining East African kanindo/soukous leads with local rhythms. |
| `central-southern-africa:17` | **Malawian Kwela-Jazz & Banjo Folk** | Blantyre & Zomba, Malawi | Acoustic homemade cuatro-banjo, pennywhistle, and one-string babatoni bass skiffle-jazz. |
| `central-southern-africa:18` | **Mozambican Marrabenta** | Maputo (Lourenço Marques), Mozambique | Sunny, syncopated southern Mozambican urban guitar dance music born from tin-can guitars and Magika/Xangana rhythms. |
| `central-southern-africa:19` | **South African Mbaqanga (Township Jive)** | Soweto & Johannesburg, South Africa | 1960s–80s urban groove pairing booming melodic electric basslines, saxophones, and accordion with deep groan-vocal (mahotella) harmonies. |
| `central-southern-africa:20` | **Isicathamiya** | KwaZulu-Natal & Johannesburg Hostels, South Africa | Unaccompanied Zulu male a cappella close-harmony singing paired with tiptoe synchronized choreography. |
| `central-southern-africa:21` | **Maskandi (Zulu Guitar Blues)** | KwaZulu-Natal, South Africa | Virtuosic fingerpicked acoustic guitar music featuring the rapid introductory 'izihlabo' flourish, concertina, violin, and rapid spoken praise (izibongo). |
| `central-southern-africa:22` | **Marabi & Cape Jazz (Ghoema)** | Johannesburg (Sophiatown) & Cape Town, South Africa | Three-chord township keyboard/organ jazz and Cape Malay carnival 'ghoema' drum jazz driven by Abdullah Ibrahim and Chris McGregor. |
| `central-southern-africa:23` | **Kwela** | Sophiatown, Johannesburg, South Africa | 1950s street-corner pennywhistle skiffle-jazz propelled by swinging banjo, acoustic guitar, and tea-chest bass. |
| `central-southern-africa:24` | **South African Bubblegum (80s Township Synth-Boogie)** | Johannesburg, South Africa | 1980s urban dance-pop marrying mbaqanga slap-bass and call-and-response vocals with Yamaha DX7 synth brass and LinnDrums. |
| `central-southern-africa:25` | **Kwaito** | Soweto, Johannesburg, South Africa | Early-1990s post-apartheid youth house music played at slowed-down ~95–110 BPM tempos with looped township chants in Isicamtho slang. |
| `central-southern-africa:26` | **Shangaan Electro** | Limpopo & Soweto, South Africa | 180+ BPM hyper-kinetic electronic dance music created by Nozinja, pairing marimba-like keyboard presets with traditional Tsonga vocals. |
| `central-southern-africa:27` | **Amapiano** | Pretoria & Gauteng Townships, South Africa | 2010s–present deep house hybrid (~112 BPM) defined by the percussive, pitching 'log drum' sub-bass synth, jazz Rhodes chords, and shakers. |
| `central-southern-africa:28` | **Gqom** | Durban, KwaZulu-Natal, South Africa | Dark, minimalist, broken-beat 125–130 BPM club music featuring hypnotic tom-drum patterns, industrial sirens, and sparse chants. |
| `central-southern-africa:29` | **Basotho Famo** | Maseru (Lesotho) & Free State Mines | Accordion (koriana) and oil-drum bass music accompanied by lengthy Sesotho poetic recitations (sefela). |
| `central-southern-africa:30` | **San / Ju/'hoansi Healing Trance Polyphony & Musical Bow** | Kalahari Desert (Botswana & Namibia) | Interlocking yodeled vocal hockets, handclapping polyrhythms, and mouth-resonated hunting bow overtones. |

| `middle-east:07` | **Aleppo Muwashshah & Qudud Halabiya** | Aleppo, Syria | Ornate urban classical and light-classical vocal suites set to strophic Andalusian poetry and intricate maqam rhythmic cycles (usuls). |
| `middle-east:08` | **Palestinian Dahiyya & Yarghoul Wedding Chant** | Negev, Hebron & West Bank, Palestine | Bedouin and rural Palestinian nocturnal line-dance music featuring rhythmic handclaps, guttural respiratory grunts, and double-pipe yarghoul. |
| `middle-east:09` | **Iraqi Maqam (Baghdadi Chalghi Ensemble)** | Baghdad & Mosul, Iraq | Highly structured urban vocal maqam repertoire accompanied by the santur (hammered dulcimer), joza (spiked fiddle), dumbuk, and riqq. |
| `middle-east:10` | **Iraqi Choubi & Basra Khashaba** | Rural Central Iraq & Basra Port | Driving rural line-dance music (choubi) and polyrhythmic Basra port clapping-and-khashaba drum music with oud and kawala flute. |
| `middle-east:11` | **Kuwaiti & Bahraini Sawt** | Kuwait City & Muharraq, Bahrain | Urban maritime pearl-diving chamber music featuring the oud, mirwas (small double-headed drums), violin, and syncopated handclapping (sharbuka). |
| `middle-east:12` | **Khaleeji Liwa & Tanbura (Nuban)** | Oman, UAE, Kuwait, Bahrain & Eastern Saudi Arabia | Afro-Gulf maritime trance and circle-dance traditions powered by the mizmar/surnay oboe, three barrel drums, or the six-string manjur/tanbura lyre. |
| `middle-east:13` | **Omani Bara'a & Razha** | Dhofar & Muscat, Oman | Sword-and-dagger ceremonial warrior dances accompanied by kasir and rahmani drums and antiphonal male poetry. |
| `middle-east:14` | **Yemeni Sana'ani Song (Al-Ghina al-Sana'ani)** | Sana'a, Yemen | Ancient poetic song tradition where a solo vocalist accompanies themselves on the four-course Yemeni qanbus lute or hammered copper sahn plate. |
| `middle-east:15` | **Hadhrami Dan & Zamil** | Wadi Hadhramaut & Mukalla, Southern Yemen | Improvised poetic nocturnal gatherings combining microtonal vocal melodies, handclapping, oud, and syncopated Gulf-Indian Ocean rhythms. |
| `middle-east:16` | **Saudi Hijazi Mizmar & Yanbu'awi Simsimiyya** | Jeddah, Mecca & Yanbu (Red Sea Coast), Saudi Arabia | Red Sea port music featuring the plucked twenty-string Yanbu simsimiyya lyre or fiery Hijazi woodwind-and-drum fire dances. |
| `middle-east:17` | **Jordanian Samer & Bedouin Rababa Poetry** | Wadi Rum, Karak & Badia, Jordan | Single-string horsehair rababa fiddle recitations and antiphonal wedding line-chants (al-samer). |
| `middle-east:18` | **Iranian Radif / Dastgah Classical Music** | Tehran, Isfahan & Shiraz, Iran | Modal Persian art music built on twelve dastgahs, featuring tar, setar, kamancheh, santur, ney, tombak, and avian-like tahrir vocal trills. |
| `middle-east:19` | **Iranian Bandari** | Hormozgan & Bushehr (Persian Gulf Coast), Southern Iran | Infectious 6/8 southern Iranian coastal dance music driven by ney-anban (gulf bagpipe), dammam drums, and Afro-Arab-Iranian syncopation. |
| `middle-east:20` | **Pre-1979 Tehrooni Kafei (Motrebi) & Persian Funk-Pop** | Lalehzar Street, Tehran, Iran | 1960s–70s Iranian nightclub soul, surf-guitar psych, and cinematic disco-funk blending Persian vocal modes with Hammond organ and brass. |
| `middle-east:21` | **Kurdish Dengbêj & Tembûr Music** | Diyarbakır, Dersim, Hewlêr (Erbil) & Mahabad | Unaccompanied epic historical vocal storytelling (dengbêj) and sacred Yarsani/Alevi long-necked tembûr lute music. |
| `middle-east:22` | **Armenian Duduk Drone & Ashugh Minstrel Song** | Yerevan, Gyumri & Lori, Armenia | Double-reed apricot-wood duduk music where a velvet microtonal lead floats over a continuous circular-breathed second duduk drone (dam). |
| `middle-east:23` | **Armenian Rabiz & Kef Time Music** | Yerevan, Armenia & Diaspora | Urban street and wedding music blending Armenian folk melodies and Mugham-style ornamentation with accordion, clarinet, and synth leads. |
| `middle-east:24` | **Georgian Three-Part Polyphonic Song (Krimanchuli & Chakrulo)** | Guria, Kakheti & Svaneti, Georgia | UNESCO-recognized non-tertian three-voice vocal polyphony featuring high yodeled countertenor (krimanchuli) or long Kakhetian drones. |
| `middle-east:25` | **Georgian Panduri & Dol-Garmoni Dance Music** | Tbilisi & Khevsureti, Georgia | Brisk 6/8 highland dance music pairing the three-string fretted panduri lute, Caucasian garmoni accordion, and double-headed doli drum. |
| `middle-east:26` | **Azerbaijani Mugham** | Baku, Shusha (Karabakh) & Shirvan, Azerbaijan | Improvisational modal art form uniting a soaring khananda vocalist (with daf frame drum), double-chambered tar lute, and bowed kamancha. |
| `middle-east:27` | **Azerbaijani Vağzalı & Jazz-Mugham** | Baku, Azerbaijan | Mid-20th-century fusion pioneered by Vagif Mustafazadeh combining American bebop piano harmonies with microtonal Azerbaijani mugham improvisation. |
| `middle-east:28` | **Azerbaijani Meyxana** | Baku & Absheron Villages, Azerbaijan | Improvised, rapid-fire rhythmic spoken-word and finger-snapping poetic duels performed at weddings and gatherings. |
| `middle-east:29` | **Chechen & Dagestani Lezginka (Zikr & Dechig Pondar)** | Grozny (Chechnya) & Makhachkala (Dagestan), North Caucasus | High-tempo 6/8 North Caucasian eagle-dance music featuring Caucasian accordion, барабан (doli) snare-hand-drum, and Sufi circle zikr. |
| `middle-east:30` | **Circassian (Adyghe) Shichepshin & Pshina Dance Music** | Maykop, Nalchik & Krasnodar (Northwest Caucasus) | Aristocratic North Caucasian dance suites played on the bowed two-string shichepshin fiddle, wooden clappers (pkhatsik), and button accordion. |

# Central Asia

## Research leads

| Route ID | Source label | Claimed origin / scene — verify | Claimed sonic profile — verify |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `central-asia:01` | **Uzbek & Tajik Shashmaqom** | Bukhara & Samarkand (Uzbekistan) / Dushanbe (Tajikistan) | Six-suite classical court music combining Sufi ghazal poetry in Uzbek and Tajik with tanbur, dutar, sato (bowed tanbur), and doira frame drum. |
| `central-asia:02` | **Khorezm Lazgi & Suvora** | Urgench & Khiva (Khorezm Oasis), Uzbekistan | Exuberant oasis dance music featuring trembling shoulder/finger movements, bulamon double-reed pipe, accordion (garmon), and tar. |
| `central-asia:03` | **Soviet Uzbek Disco-Funk & Tashkent Estrada (Yalla Era)** | Tashkent, Uzbekistan | 1970s–80s silk-road psychedelic pop-funk blending electric rubab and doira rhythms with Moog synthesizers, fuzz guitars, and strings. |
| `central-asia:04` | **Fergana Valley Katta Ashula (Patnis Ashula)** | Kokand, Margilan & Andijan, Uzbekistan | High-register outdoor duet singing where vocalists hold brass trays (patnis) in front of their mouths to direct sound and monitor resonance. |
| `central-asia:05` | **Tajik Pamiri Maddoh & Falak** | Gorno-Badakhshan (Pamir Mountains) & Kulyab, Tajikistan | Devotional Ismaili mystical poetry sung with Pamiri rubab and daf (maddoh) alongside intense highland crying-melody laments (falak). |
| `central-asia:06` | **Tajik Folktronica & Dushanbe Synth-Folk** | Dushanbe, Tajikistan | Late-Soviet and post-independence fusion pairing Pamiri seven-string setar and daf rhythms with analog drum machines and ambient synths. |
| `central-asia:07` | **Kazakh Küi (Dombra Instrumental Narrative)** | Almaty, Atyrau & Kazakh Steppe, Kazakhstan | Virtuosic programmatic solo pieces for the two-string fretless/fretted dombra lute using percussive right-hand strumming (tökpe) or fingerpicking (shertpe). |
| `central-asia:08` | **Kazakh Qobyz Shamanic Overtones** | Syr Darya & Central Steppe, Kazakhstan | Ancient bowed two-string horsehair fiddle (qyl-qobyz) music producing raspy, wolf-howl and swan-call harmonic overtones. |
| `central-asia:09` | **Q-Pop (Qazaq Pop) & Steppe Hip-Hop** | Almaty & Astana, Kazakhstan | Contemporary Kazakh-language pop and trap combining modern K-pop/hip-hop production with Kazakh dombra motifs and steppe lyrical themes. |
| `central-asia:10` | **Kyrgyz Manas Epic Recitation & Komuz Küü** | Bishkek, Issyk-Kul & Naryn, Kyrgyzstan | Unaccompanied rhythmic chanting of the half-million-line Epic of Manas and acrobatic three-string apricot-wood komuz lute instrumentals. |
| `central-asia:11` | **Kyrgyz Temir Komuz (Jaw Harp) Ensemble** | Bishkek & Tien Shan Mountains, Kyrgyzstan | Synchronized iron and wooden jaw-harp ensembles creating rapid polyrhythmic overtone melodies and galloping horse rhythms. |
| `central-asia:12` | **Turkmen Bakhshi Dutar & Gijak Music** | Ashgabat, Mary & Dashoguz, Turkmenistan | Intense, guttural Turkmen bard singing accompanied by the two-string mulberry-wood dutar and spiked three-string gijak fiddle. |
| `central-asia:13` | **Turkmen Kushtdepdi Zikr-Dance Chant** | Balkan Province (Caspian Coast), Turkmenistan | Whirling coastal circle-dance music driven by rhythmic vocal grunts ('uh-hu, kusht!'), handclaps, and accelerating breath percussion. |
| `central-asia:14` | **Uyghur Twelve Muqam (On Ikki Muqam)** | Kashgar, Hotan, Yarkand & Turpan (Tarim Basin) | Monumental twelve-suite oasis classical tradition played on rawap, satar (bowed lute with sympathetic strings), tambur, chang dulcimer, and dap. |
| `central-asia:15` | **Uyghur Dolan Muqam & Meshrep** | Makit, Maralbexi & Awat (Taklamakan Desert Edge) | Raw, ecstatic desert community harvest music featuring guttural vocals, Dolan rawap, Dolan ghijak, and group circle dancing. |
| `central-asia:16` | **Uyghur Rock & Tarim Electric Guitar** | Ürümqi & Kashgar | 1990s–present rock and flamenco-inflected electric guitar music adapting compound 5/8 and 7/8 Uyghur meshrep rhythms. |
| `central-asia:17` | **Karakalpak Jyrau (Kobyz Bardic Epic)** | Nukus & Aral Sea Region, Karakalpakstan | Throat-rasping heroic epic singing performed by jyrau bards playing the horsehair kobyz or dutar. |
| `central-asia:18` | **Afghan Kabuli Ghazal & Kharabat Art Music** | Kharabat Quarter, Kabul, Afghanistan | Courtly Afghan vocal art blending Persian poetry with North Indian Hindustani raga theory, tabla, harmonium, and Afghan rubab. |
| `central-asia:19` | **Afghan Rubab Classical & Logari Folk** | Kabul & Logar Valley, Afghanistan | Resonant, short-necked 18-string carved mulberry lute music featuring dynamic 'paranda' right-hand rhythmic strokes and sudden stops. |
| `central-asia:20` | **Herati Dutār & Chaharbeiti** | Herat, Western Afghanistan | Long-necked 14-string steel-strung Herati dutar music accompanying melancholic four-line folk quatrains. |
| `central-asia:21` | **Pashto Landay & Attan Dance Music** | Kandahar, Jalalabad & Khyber Pakhtunkhwa | Two-line women's oral poetry (landay) and accelerating dhol-and-surnai drum-and-shawm circle-dance music (attan). |
| `central-asia:22` | **Hazaragi Dambura Song** | Bamyan & Central Highlands (Hazarajat), Afghanistan | Two-string fretless wooden lute (dambura) music played with percussive finger-slaps on the soundboard and high-register vocal melodies. |
| `central-asia:23` | **70s Radio Afghanistan Psych-Pop & Ahmad Zahir Era** | Kabul, Afghanistan | Golden-age 1960s–70s Kabul studio music combining Farfisa organs, trumpet, and surf guitar with tabla and Afghan rubab. |
| `central-asia:24` | **Bukharan Jewish Shashmaqom & Mavrigi** | Bukhara & Samarkand, Uzbekistan | High-energy polyrhythmic Bukharan wedding suite tradition (mavrigi) driven by multiple doira frame drums and Tajik/Judeo-Tajik vocals. |
| `central-asia:25` | **Nuristani Vaj Harp & High-Mountain Polyphony** | Nuristan (Hindu Kush Mountains), Northeastern Afghanistan | Archaic four-to-six-string bow-harp (vaj/waji) music accompanied by clapped polyrhythms and dissonant vocal intervals. |
| `central-asia:26` | **Badakhshani Ghijak & Daf Dance** | Feyzabad (Afghan Badakhshan) & Khorog | High-altitude Amu Darya borderland dance music played on tin-can or wooden ghijak fiddles, Pamiri rubab, and bells. |
| `central-asia:27` | **Yaghnobi & Zeravshan Mountain Flute Music** | Yaghnob Valley (Sughd Province), Tajikistan | Ancient Sogdian-descended pastoral flute (nay-i chupani) and women's frame-drum songs from remote Zeravshan valleys. |
| `central-asia:28` | **Kazakh & Kyrgyz Aitys (Improvised Dombra/Komuz Duel)** | Almaty, Shymkent & Osh | Live competitive sung-poetry battles where two bards trade witty social satire while strumming dombra or komuz. |
| `central-asia:29` | **Dungan Folk Song (Central Asian Hui Tradition)** | Chui Valley (Kyrgyzstan) & Masanchi (Kazakhstan) | Gansu/Shaanxi-derived pentatonic narrative songs preserved along the Chu River with sanxian and erhu. |
| `central-asia:30` | **Khorasani Dotar & Bakhshi Epic** | Bojnord, Quchan & Torbat-e Jam (Greater Khorasan) | Trance-like two-string dotar lute music bridging Persian, Kurdish (Kurmanji), and Turkmen bardic traditions. |

| Route ID | Source label | Claimed origin / scene — verify | Claimed sonic profile — verify |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `south-asia:01` | **Qawwali (Sufi Devotional Ensemble)** | Punjab & Sindh (Pakistan) / Delhi & Uttar Pradesh (India) | Ecstatic Sufi devotional music driven by lead/chorus call-and-response, harmonium, dholak/tabla, and synchronized ensemble handclapping. |
| `south-asia:02` | **Hindustani Khayal & Dhrupad** | North India (Gwalior, Agra, Jaipur, Kirana, Patiala Gharanas) | Improvisational raga-and-tala art music progressing from unpulsed microtonal alap to rapid tanas over tabla or pakhawaj. |
| `south-asia:03` | **Carnatic Kriti & Konnakol** | Chennai, Thanjavur & Tiruvarur (Tamil Nadu / South India) | South Indian classical music featuring gamaka vocal ornamentation, violin, mridangam, ghatam clay pot, and spoken konnakol rhythm syllables. |
| `south-asia:04` | **Baul Mystic Song** | Birbhum & Nadia (West Bengal, India) / Kushtia (Bangladesh) | Wandering syncretic minstrel music played on the one-string gourd ektara, dotara, duggi kettle drum, and ankle bells. |
| `south-asia:05` | **Bhatiali & Bhawaiya River/Cartmen Songs** | Brahmaputra & Padma River Basins (Bangladesh & Assam/North Bengal) | Long-drawn, undulating vocal melodies of boatmen (bhatiali) and vocal-break dotara songs of bullock-cart drivers (bhawaiya). |
| `south-asia:06` | **Punjabi Bhangra & Tumbi-Algoza Folk** | Majha, Malwa & Doaba (Punjab, India & Pakistan) | Harvest dance music powered by the booming double-sided dhol drum, high-pitched single-string tumbi, and paired algoza flutes. |
| `south-asia:07` | **Rajasthani Manganiyar & Langha Kamaicha Music** | Thar Desert (Jaisalmer, Barmer & Jodhpur), Rajasthan, India | Hereditary desert musician tradition featuring the seventeen-string bowed kamaicha, sindhi sarangi, khartal wooden clappers, and dholak. |
| `south-asia:08` | **Balochi Zahirok & Sujawal Sorud (Suzawan)** | Makran Coast & Turbat (Balochistan, Pakistan / Iran) | Deeply meditative modal trance music centered on the bowed double-bellied suroz fiddle, benju (keyed zither), and dambiro lute. |
| `south-asia:09` | **Sindhi Kafi & Shah Jo Raag** | Bhit Shah & Sehwan Sharif, Sindh, Pakistan | Sufi shrine music sung in falsetto voice accompanied by the five-stringed danburo lute, yaktaro, and clay gharo pitcher percussion. |
| `south-asia:10` | **Kashmiri Sufiana Mousiqui & Chakri** | Srinagar, Kashmir | Persian-rooted Kashmiri classical chamber music played on the hundred-stringed santoor and rabab, alongside folk chakri with tumbaknari clay drums. |
| `south-asia:11` | **Goan Mando & Dekhni** | Salcete & Tiswadi, Goa, India | Lusophone-Konkani 6/8 ballroom and folk music combining Portuguese violin and guitar counter-melodies with the clay ghumot drum. |
| `south-asia:12` | **Mumbai Jazz-Age & Bollywood Crime-Funk (R.D. Burman Era)** | Mumbai (Bombay), Maharashtra, India | 1960s–70s Hindi cinema soundtrack fusion colliding surf guitar, Moog synthesizers, and brass funk with 60-piece string sections and tabla. |
| `south-asia:13` | **Lollywood Disco & 80s Lahore Synth-Pop** | Lahore & Karachi, Pakistan | Late-1970s–80s Pakistani film and cassette disco blending Italo-style synthesizer basslines and drum machines with Urdu vocal ghazal phrasing. |
| `south-asia:14` | **South Asian Pedal/Lap Steel Guitar Raga & Film Interpretations** | Kolkata & Mumbai, India | Mid-20th-century adaptation of Hawaiian lap steel guitar (Taufiq/Van Shipley/Brij Bhushan Kabra tradition) to slide-glissando ragas and film themes. |
| `south-asia:15` | **Hindi Independent Rock & Folk-Fusion** | New Delhi, Mumbai & Shillong, India | 1990s–present independent Indian band movement blending Hindi/Hindustani folk vocals and tabla with progressive rock and jazz-fusion guitars. |
| `south-asia:16` | **Tamil Gaana & Dappankuthu** | North Chennai (Royapuram / Kasimedu), Tamil Nadu, India | Working-class Dalit street and funeral percussion music driven by raw parai/tappu frame drums, witty urban slang, and high-energy brass. |
| `south-asia:17` | **Kerala Panchavadyam & Chenda Melam** | Thrissur & Palakkad, Kerala, South India | Monumental temple percussion orchestras of up to 150 musicians playing cylindrical chenda drums, maddalam, ilathalam cymbals, and kombu horns. |
| `south-asia:18` | **Mappila Paattu** | Malabar Coast (Kozhikode & Malappuram), Kerala, India | Arab-Malayalam Muslim coastal song tradition blending Arabic maqam-inflected melodies with Keralan rhythms and harmonium/tabla. |
| `south-asia:19` | **Marathi Lavani & Powada** | Pune, Kolhapur & Solapur, Maharashtra, India | Fast, seductive nine-yard-saree dance music driven by the dholki barrel drum (lavani) and heroic historical balladry (powada). |
| `south-asia:20` | **Assamese Bihu** | Brahmaputra Valley, Assam, Northeast India | Vernal equinox festival music powered by the buffalo-horn pepa pipe, dhol, gogona bamboo jaw harp, and tokas. |
| `south-asia:21` | **Manipuri Sankirtana & Pena Music** | Imphal Valley, Manipur, Northeast India | Acrobatic cymbal-and-pung-drum Vaishnavite dance percussion and ancient Meitei coconut-shell bowed pena fiddle songs. |
| `south-asia:22` | **Nepali Dohori & Gandharva Sarangi Song** | Pokhara & Kathmandu Valley, Nepal | Improvised male-female flirting song duels over madal drum (dohori) and itinerant Gaine/Gandharva four-string sarangi ballads. |
| `south-asia:23` | **Newar Dapha & Dhime Percussion** | Bhaktapur, Patan & Kathmandu, Nepal | Complex polyrhythmic Kathmandu Valley temple music featuring heavy dhime drums, bhusya cymbals, and seasonal raga-based dapha hymns. |
| `south-asia:24` | **Sri Lankan Baila (Kaffrinha)** | Colombo, Negombo & Batticaloa, Sri Lanka | Infectious 6/8 Afro-Portuguese-Sinhala party music combining rabana frame drums, accordion, banjo, brass, and calypso-like bounce. |
| `south-asia:25` | **Sri Lankan Viridu & Kandyan Vannam** | Kandy, Sri Lanka | Improvised rabana-tambourine narrative poetry (viridu) and classical Kandyan dance suites played on geta bera drum and horanewa oboe. |
| `south-asia:26` | **Dhivehi Boduberu** | Malé & Atolls, Maldives | East African-rooted Maldivian island drumming tradition where fifteen musicians build coconut-log drum rhythms from a slow pulse to a frenzy. |
| `south-asia:27` | **Bhutanese Zhungdra & Boedra (Dramyin Music)** | Thimphu & Paro, Bhutan | High-Himalayan monastic vocal modes (zhungdra) and courtly songs accompanied by the seven-stringed dragon-headed dramyin lute and chiwang. |
| `south-asia:28` | **Gilgit-Baltistan Dani & Surnai-Dadang Music** | Hunza, Gilgit & Skardu (Karakoram Mountains), Northern Pakistan | High-mountain Shina, Burushaski, and Wakhi reed-pipe (surnai), kettle-drum (damal), and rubab sword-dance music. |
| `south-asia:29` | **Chhattisgarhi Pandavani** | Durg & Raipur, Chhattisgarh, Central India | Solo theatrical balladry where a singer narrates the Mahabharata while using a decorated tambura lute as both prop and drone. |
| `south-asia:30` | **Bhojpuri Biraha & Chutney-Folk** | Varanasi & Bhojpur (Eastern Uttar Pradesh & Western Bihar), India | High-pitched narrative folk songs driven by the metallic jori-kartal clappers, dholak, and harmonium. |

| `east-asia:01` | **Japanese City Pop** | Tokyo, Japan | Late-1970s–80s affluent urban pop-boogie fusing American AOR, jazz-fusion, slap bass, Roland Juno synths, and lush studio horn arrangements. |
| `east-asia:02` | **Kankyō Ongaku (Japanese Environmental & Ambient Music)** | Tokyo, Japan | 1980s Japanese interior architecture and minimalist sound-design music crafted on Roland synthesizers, marimbas, and acoustic percussion. |
| `east-asia:03` | **Shibuya-kei** | Shibuya, Tokyo, Japan | 1990s hyper-referential cut-and-paste indie pop blending 1960s French yé-yé, bossa nova, sunshine pop, jazz breaks, and indie guitars. |
| `east-asia:04` | **Japanese Group Sounds (GS) & 60s Eleki-Surf** | Tokyo & Yokohama, Japan | Mid-to-late-1960s Japanese fuzz-garage and Mosrite-guitar surf rock fusing pentatonic kayōkyoku melodies with British Invasion beat. |
| `east-asia:05` | **Japanoise (Onkyo & Harsh Noise)** | Kyoto, Osaka & Tokyo, Japan | Late-1970s–90s extreme experimental noise movement pushing feedback loops, contact microphones, and pedal chains to physical limits. |
| `east-asia:06` | **Enka** | Tokyo & Osaka, Japan | Sentimental postwar Japanese balladry sung with wide vibrato and 'kobushi' vocal melisma over yonanuki pentatonic scales and electric guitar. |
| `east-asia:07` | **Okinawan Min'yō & Shima-Uta (Sanshin Music)** | Okinawa & Ryukyu Islands, Japan | Subtropical island music built on the joyful Ryukyuan major scale (do-mi-fa-sol-ti) played on the snakeskin three-string sanshin lute. |
| `east-asia:08` | **Tsugaru-jamisen** | Tsugaru Peninsula (Aomori Prefecture), Northern Japan | Improvisational, percussive northern Japanese shamisen style striking the dog/cat-skin membrane with a heavy bachi plectrum. |
| `east-asia:09` | **Gagaku** | Kyoto & Tokyo Imperial Court, Japan | Millennium-old Japanese imperial court orchestra music characterized by mouth-organ (shō) tone clusters, hichiriki oboe, and slow dynamic swells. |
| `east-asia:10` | **Ainu Upopo & Mukkuri Music** | Hokkaido, Northern Japan | Indigenous Ainu polyphonic canon singing (ukouk) and bamboo jaw-harp (mukkuri) and five-string tonkori zither pieces. |
| `east-asia:11` | **Korean Pansori** | Jeolla Province & Seoul, South Korea | Dramatic solo musical storytelling where one vocalist (sorikkun) with a folding fan and one barrel drummer (gosu) perform multi-hour epics. |
| `east-asia:12` | **Korean Sanjo (Gayageum & Geomungo Solo)** | South Korea | Improvisational 'scattered melodies' for solo silk-stringed zither accompanied by janggu hourglass drum, accelerating from slow jinyangjo to danmori. |
| `east-asia:13` | **Samulnori & Pungmul Percussion** | Seoul & Rural South Korea | Four-instrument Korean percussion quartet (kkwaenggwari small gong, jing large gong, janggu drum, buk barrel drum) playing complex compound meters. |
| `east-asia:14` | **Korean Sinawi & Shamanic Gut Music** | Jindo Island & Gyeonggi, South Korea | Improvisational ensemble music accompanying shamanic rituals, featuring sorrowful piri oboe, daegeum flute, and haegeum fiddle. |
| `east-asia:15` | **1970s Korean Psychedelic Folk-Rock (Shin Joong-hyun Era)** | Seoul, South Korea | Late-1960s–70s South Korean acid-rock and soul forged around US Eighth Army clubs and pentatonic fuzz-guitar psychedelia. |
| `east-asia:16` | **Trot (Ppongjjak)** | Seoul & Busan, South Korea | Duple-meter ('쿵짝 / kung-jjak') Korean popular song style combining expressive vocal vibrato with brass, accordion, or disco synths. |
| `east-asia:17` | **Chinese Jiangnan Sizhu (Silk and Bamboo)** | Shanghai, Suzhou & Hangzhou (Yangtze Delta), China | Heterophonic tea-house chamber music where erhu fiddle, pipa lute, dizi flute, and yangqin dulcimer ornament the same melody simultaneously. |
| `east-asia:18` | **Guqin Literati Solo Music** | Suzhou, Beijing & Sichuan, China | Contemplative seven-string fretless zither tradition emphasizing subtle left-hand string glides, harmonics, and silence. |
| `east-asia:19` | **Kunqu & Beijing Opera (Jingju)** | Suzhou & Beijing, China | Classical Chinese musical theatre driven by piercing jinghu two-string fiddle, wooden clappers (ban), and gongs. |
| `east-asia:20` | **Shaanbei Xintianyou & Qinqiang** | Yan'an & Xi'an (Loess Plateau, Shaanxi), Northwestern China | High-pitched, soaring canyon folk songs (xintianyou) and roaring woodblock-driven Northwestern opera (qinqiang). |
| `east-asia:21` | **Cantonese Music (Guangdong Yinyue) & Cantopop** | Guangzhou (Canton) & Hong Kong | Bright, bouncy Pearl River Delta instrumental ensemble music (gaohu, qinqin, yangqin) and Hong Kong's cosmopolitan studio pop heritage. |
| `east-asia:22` | **Fujian Nanyin (Nanguan)** | Quanzhou & Xiamen (Southern Fujian), China & Taiwan | Slow, archaic Tang/Song-era southern chamber music featuring the horizontally held crooked-neck pipa, dongxiao flute, and clappers. |
| `east-asia:23` | **Chaozhou Xianshi & Gong-Drum Music** | Shantou & Chaozhou (Eastern Guangdong), China | Teochew string-poetry chamber music utilizing microtonal 'heavy-six / light-six' modal variations on the erxian fiddle and guzheng. |
| `east-asia:24` | **Chinese Yaogun & Beijing Post-Punk (No Beijing Wave)** | Beijing & Wuhan, China | 1980s Cui Jian-era Chinese rock and 2000s Beijing underground post-punk/noise-rock blending angular guitars with urban alienation. |
| `east-asia:25` | **Taiwanese Nakashi & Hokkien Enka-Pop** | Beitou (Taipei) & Tainan, Taiwan | Itinerant hot-spring tavern accordion-and-guitar music (nakashi) that shaped classic Taiwanese Hokkien-language pop ballads. |
| `east-asia:26` | **Taiwanese Indigenous Polyphonic Song (Bunun Pasibutbut)** | Central Mountain Range & Taitung (Bunun, Amis, Paiwan), Taiwan | Austronesian microtonal eight-part chromatic rising prayer for the millet harvest (Pasibutbut) and joyful Amis coastal polyphony. |
| `east-asia:27` | **Mongolian Urtyn Duu (Long Song) & Morin Khuur** | Central Steppe & Gobi Desert, Mongolia | Vast, unmeasured vocal melodies mimicking steppe horizons, accompanied by the two-string horsehead fiddle (morin khuur). |
| `east-asia:28` | **Mongolian Khöömii (Overtone Singing)** | Khovd & Govi-Altai (Western Mongolia) | Diphonic vocal technique isolating whistling harmonic overtones above a fundamental throat drone. |
| `east-asia:29` | **Tibetan Dungchen Monastic Music & Nangma-Toeshey** | Lhasa, Kham & Amdo (Tibetan Plateau) | Sub-bass telescopic brass horns (dungchen) and overtone chanting alongside secular Lhasa tavern fiddle-and-lute (dranyen) dance songs. |
| `east-asia:30` | **Dong Grand Song (Kam Gal Laox)** | Liping & Rongjiang (Southeastern Guizhou), China | Unconducted, unaccompanied multi-part village choral polyphony imitating cicadas, mountain streams, and seasonal life. |

# END SOURCE: references/regions/east-asia.md

# Southeast Asia

| Route ID | Source label | Claimed origin / scene — verify | Claimed sonic profile — verify |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `southeast-asia:01` | **Pre-1975 Saigon Sound (Nhạc Trẻ, Surf, Soul & Psych)** | Saigon (Ho Chi Minh City), Vietnam | 1960s–1975 Vietnamese electric band scene combining twangy Fender surf guitar, Hammond organ, brass funk, and Vietnamese melodic phrasing. |
| `southeast-asia:02` | **Vietnamese Ca Trù** | Hanoi & Red River Delta, Northern Vietnam | Intimate northern Vietnamese chamber poetry sung with tense vocal ornamentation accompanied by the three-string đàn đáy lute and praise drum. |
| `southeast-asia:03` | **Vietnamese Đờn Ca Tài Tử & Cải Lương** | Mekong Delta & Ho Chi Minh City, Southern Vietnam | Southern Vietnamese chamber music and folk opera featuring the scalloped-fret đàn ghi-ta phím lõm guitar, đàn tranh zither, and song lang clapper. |
| `southeast-asia:04` | **Vietnamese Chầu Văn (Hầu Đồng Spirit Medium Music)** | Nam Định & Hanoi, Vietnam | Hypnotic, driving ritual trance music played on the two-string đàn nguyệt moon lute, bamboo phách, and syncopated drums. |
| `southeast-asia:05` | **Cambodian Golden-Era Psychedelic Rock & Romvong** | Phnom Penh, Cambodia | 1960s–early-1970s Khmer electric rock fusing fuzz guitars, Farfisa organs, and Afro-Latin rhythms with traditional Khmer vocal trills. |
| `southeast-asia:06` | **Cambodian Pinpeat & Chapei Dang Veng** | Siem Reap & Phnom Penh, Cambodia | Royal court gong-chime/xylophone/oboe orchestra (pinpeat) and witty solo two-string long-necked lute storytelling (chapei dang veng). |
| `southeast-asia:07` | **Classic Pre-1975 Lao Lam / Lum & Royal-Era Pop** | Vientiane, Luang Prabang & Champasak, Laos | Regional Lao vocal courtship traditions (lam sithandone, khap thum) and early electric band arrangements built around the khaen bamboo mouth organ. |
| `southeast-asia:08` | **Thai Molam (Mor Lam Sing & Isan Funk)** | Isan Region (Khon Kaen, Ubon Ratchathani, Roi Et), Northeastern Thailand | Fast-paced, electrified Lao-Isan music pairing hypnotic khaen mouth-organ drones and 3-string electric phin lute leads with funk rhythm sections. |
| `southeast-asia:09` | **Thai Luk Thung** | Suphan Buri & Bangkok, Central Thailand | Mid-20th-century Thai country-soul combining ornate, slow-vibrato vocal storytelling with Latin mambo brass, surf guitars, and woodwinds. |
| `southeast-asia:10` | **Thai Shadow Music (60s–70s Bangkok Instrumental Surf-Guitar)** | Bangkok, Thailand | 1960s Thai electric guitar combos inspired by The Shadows, blending spring-reverb Fender Stratocaster melodies with Thai pentatonic scales. |
| `southeast-asia:11` | **Thai Piphat Ensemble** | Bangkok & Ayutthaya, Thailand | Classical Thai percussion-and-wind orchestra led by the high-pitched ranat ek wooden xylophone, kong wong yai gong circle, and pi nai quadruple-reed oboe. |
| `southeast-asia:12` | **Burmese Hsaing Waing & Pat Waing** | Mandalay & Yangon, Myanmar | Dynamic Burmese classical orchestra centered on a 21-drum tuned circle (pat waing), hne oboe, and sudden, dramatic rhythmic shifts. |
| `southeast-asia:13` | **Burmese Myanma Gita (Saung Gauk Harp Song) & Stereo Rock** | Yangon & Mandalay, Myanmar | Arched sixteen-string silk harp (saung gauk) court songs and 1970s Yangon 'Copy Thachin / Stereo' fuzz-guitar rock. |
| `southeast-asia:14` | **Indonesian Garage Psych & 70s Pop Kreatif** | Surabaya, Jakarta & Bandung, Indonesia | 1970s Indonesian fuzz-organ hard rock (AKA, Shark Move) and lush cassette-era jazz-prog synthesis (Guruh Gipsy, Chrisye era). |
| `southeast-asia:15` | **Bandung & Jakarta DIY Punk / Hardcore** | Bandung & Jakarta, Indonesia | 1990s–present fiercely independent Indonesian underground street-punk, d-beat, and hardcore scene born in DIY tape labels and community spaces. |
| `southeast-asia:16` | **Javanese Gamelan (Karawitan)** | Surakarta (Solo) & Yogyakarta, Central Java, Indonesia | Meditative bronze metallophone and gong orchestra tuned to non-Western slendro (5-tone) and pelog (7-tone) scales with rebab and sindhen vocals. |
| `southeast-asia:17` | **Balinese Gamelan Gong Kebyar & Kecak** | Ubud, Gianyar & Denpasar, Bali, Indonesia | Explosive, lightning-fast interlocking metallophone passages ('kotekan') and 100-voice unaccompanied monkey-chant polyrhythms (kecak). |
| `southeast-asia:18` | **Sundanese Jaipongan & Kacapi Suling** | Bandung & West Java, Indonesia | Dynamic 1970s Sundanese kendang-drum and ketuk-tilu dance music (jaipongan) alongside serene boat-zither and bamboo-flute duets (kacapi suling). |
| `southeast-asia:19` | **Indonesian Dangdut & Dangdut Koplo** | Jakarta & East Java (Surabaya / Banyuwangi), Indonesia | Popular Indonesian dance music fusing Hindustani tabla/kendang syncopation, Arabic suling flute, and electric rock guitars (at 160+ BPM in Koplo). |
| `southeast-asia:20` | **Keroncong** | Jakarta (Tugu) & Solo, Indonesia | Nostalgic Lusophone-Indonesian chamber song tradition featuring interlocking cak and cuk ukuleles, cello played like a plucked drum, flute, and violin. |
| `southeast-asia:21` | **Minangkabau Talempong & Saluang** | West Sumatra (Padang & Bukittinggi), Indonesia | Matrilineal Sumatran kettle-gong polyrhythms (talempong) and circular-breathed oblique bamboo flute (saluang) songs. |
| `southeast-asia:22` | **Batak Gondang Sabangunan & Opera Batak** | Lake Toba, North Sumatra, Indonesia | Ritual tuned five-drum (taganing) and sarune oboe ensembles alongside powerful three-part North Sumatran vocal harmonies. |
| `southeast-asia:23` | **Indonesian Experimental Extended-Voice & Homemade-Instrument Metal** | Yogyakarta, Indonesia | Contemporary avant-garde movement (Senyawa / Gabber Modus Operandi) merging Javanese trance rituals and homemade bamboo/string instruments with noise and club music. |
| `southeast-asia:24` | **Malaysian Dondang Sayang & Asli Ronggeng** | Melaka & Penang, Malaysia | Peranakan (Straits Chinese) and Malay improvised pantun poetry sung over violin, accordion, two rebana drums, and tetawak gong. |
| `southeast-asia:25` | **1960s Singapore & Malaysian Pop Yeh-Yeh** | Singapore, Kuala Lumpur & Johor Bahru | Mid-1960s Malay-language beat and surf-garage movement pairing twangy electric guitars and Farfisa organs with traditional asli vocal turns. |
| `southeast-asia:26` | **Filipino Kundiman & Harana** | Manila & Luzon, Philippines | Lush, minor-to-major shifting Tagalog romantic art-song and courtship guitar serenade tradition in triple meter. |
| `southeast-asia:27` | **Manila Sound (70s Filipino Disco-Funk & Jazz-Pop)** | Manila, Philippines | 1970s bilingual Taglish studio groove blending Earth, Wind & Fire-style horn sections, Rhodes piano, and buoyant disco basslines. |
| `southeast-asia:28` | **Mindanao & Sulu Kulintang Ensemble** | Maguindanao, Maranao & Tausug (Southern Philippines) | Virtuosic row-of-eight graduated bronze bossed gongs played with two light wooden beaters alongside dabakan and agung gongs. |
| `southeast-asia:29` | **Pinoy Rock & Tunog Kalye** | Quezon City & Manila, Philippines | 1970s–90s Tagalog folk-rock, blues-psych, and university alternative rock chronicling everyday Manila street life. |
| `southeast-asia:30` | **Timorese Tebe-Dai & Likurai** | Dili & Bobonaro, Timor-Leste | Circle-dance chants and women's small underarm babadok drum and dadir bell music celebrating community resilience. |

## Research leads

| Route ID | Source label | Claimed origin / scene — verify | Claimed sonic profile — verify |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `northern-asia:01` | **Tuvan Khöömei (Sygyt, Kargyraa & Borbangnadyr)** | Kyzyl & Tannu-Ola Mountains, Tuva, Southern Siberia | World-renowned throat-singing tradition isolating flute-like overtones (sygyt), sub-octave undertow growls (kargyraa), and water-rolling trills. |
| `northern-asia:02` | **Tuvan Igil & Doshpuluur Punk-Folk (Yat-Kha / Huun-Huur-Tu Scene)** | Kyzyl, Tuva | Electrified Tuvan rock and folk combining horsehead igil fiddle, plucked doshpuluur lute, and kargyraa vocals with punk/post-punk guitars. |
| `northern-asia:03` | **Sakha (Yakut) Toyuk & Khomus Virtuosity** | Yakutsk & Lena River Basin, Sakha Republic, Northeastern Siberia | Improvised vocal vibrato singing (kylysakh) and rapid-fire iron jaw-harp (khomus) mimicry of arctic winds, reindeer, and tundra birds. |
| `northern-asia:04` | **Sakha Olonkho Heroic Epic & Osuokhai Circle Dance** | Central Sakha (Yakutia), Siberia | Archaic Turkic epic storytelling where a solo narrator shifts vocal timbres for heroes and spirits, alongside summer solstice osuokhai round dances. |
| `northern-asia:05` | **Buryat Yohor & Morin Khuur Steppe Song** | Ulan-Ude & Lake Baikal Region, Buryatia, Siberia | Mongolic Lake Baikal nocturnal circle-dance chants (yohor) and ornate pentatonic limbe-flute and sukhur-fiddle ballads. |
| `northern-asia:06` | **Altai Kai (Khai) Epic Throat Singing & Topshuur** | Gorno-Altaysk & Altai Mountains, Southern Siberia | Deep guttural overtone epic recitation (kai) lasting whole nights, accompanied by the two-string cedar topshuur lute and ikili fiddle. |
| `northern-asia:07` | **Khakas Khai & Chatkhan Zither Music** | Abakan & Minusinsk Hollow, Khakassia, Siberia | Steppe bardic music centered on the movable-bridge carved wooden chatkhan zither and two-register throat singing. |
| `northern-asia:08` | **Siberian Punk (Omsk / Novosibirsk Underground)** | Omsk, Novosibirsk & Tyumen, Western Siberia | Mid-1980s ferocious, distorted apartment-tape garage punk (Grazhdanskaya Oborona / Yanka Dyagileva) blending Russian folk fatalism with noise-rock. |
| `northern-asia:09` | **Chukchi Pich'einen (Throat Game) & Yarar Frame Drum** | Chukotka Peninsula, Russian Far East | Arctic coastal Indigenous music featuring walrus-stomach membrane frame drums (yarar) and competitive guttural vocal breathing games. |
| `northern-asia:10` | **Koryak & Itelmen Khalligud & Tambourine Dance** | Kamchatka Peninsula, Russian Far East | Volcanic peninsula seal-and-raven mimicry dances accompanied by resonant wooden-rimmed frame drums and rattle belts. |
| `northern-asia:11` | **Evenki (Tungus) Ikevun & Reindeer Herding Song** | Evenkia & Taiga Belt, Central/Eastern Siberia | Boreal forest nomadic songs using syllabic vocables and pentatonic motifs to guide reindeer across the Siberian taiga. |
| `northern-asia:12` | **Even Hedje Circle Dance** | Magadan, Chukotka & Northern Sakha, Russian Far East | Accelerating clockwise communal circle dance built on rhythmic throat-calls ('khe-de!'), foot steps, and shamanic drum rhythms. |
| `northern-asia:13` | **Nivkh (Gilyak) Tyngryn (One-String Fiddle) & Bear-Festival Log Percussion** | Lower Amur River & Sakhalin Island, Russian Far East | Paleosiberian ritual music featuring the carved spruce log drum (zaŝ) beaten with sticks and the birchbark-bellied tyngryn fiddle. |
| `northern-asia:14` | **Nanai (Goldi) Kaza Taori & Kunkai Jaw Harp** | Khabarovsk & Amur River Basin, Russian Far East | Amur River shamanic singing through cylindrical megaphones and delicate bamboo/brass jaw-harp solos. |
| `northern-asia:15` | **Udege & Ulch Kongokto & Reed-Pipe Music** | Sikhote-Alin Mountains (Primorsky Krai), Russian Far East | Forest hunter-gatherer music imitating woodland acoustics with overtone flutes, mouth bows, and skin drums. |
| `northern-asia:16` | **Khanty & Mansi Tor-Sapel-Yukh (Crane Harp) & Bear-Feast Song** | Ob River Basin (Yugra), Northwestern Siberia | Ob-Ugric sacred music played on the five-string boat-shaped zither (nars-yukh) and angular swan-necked harp (tor-sapel-yukh). |
| `northern-asia:17` | **Nenets Syudbabts & Yarabts Tundra Epics** | Yamal Peninsula & Taymyr, Arctic Siberia | Unaccompanied Samoyedic tundra narrative chanting where a lead bard and repeating assistant ('teltena') recount arctic survival epics. |
| `northern-asia:18` | **Nganasan Kintu & Balyk-Tur Tundra Vocal Game** | Taymyr Peninsula (Northernmost Eurasia), Siberia | High-Arctic allegorical vocal riddles and breathy throat-games performed during the polar night. |
| `northern-asia:19` | **Shor & Teleut Kai-Chörchök Epic** | Kuznetsk Alatau (Mountain Shoria), Kemerovo, Southern Siberia | Taiga-smith Turkic throat-sung legends accompanied by two-stringed willow-wood kai-komus lutes. |
| `northern-asia:20` | **Tofalar & Todzhu Reindeer-Camp Song** | Eastern Sayan Mountains, Southern Siberia | Remote Sayan highland herding melodies utilizing narrow-range pentatonic motifs and wooden Jew's harps (charty-komus). |
| `northern-asia:21` | **Siberian Post-Punk & Coldwave (Novosibirsk / Krasnoyarsk Scene)** | Novosibirsk, Krasnoyarsk & Irkutsk, Siberia | Post-Soviet Siberian minimal synth and darkwave pairing frostbitten drum-machine pulses with jangly, reverb-soaked bass guitars. |
| `northern-asia:22` | **Vladivostok Mumiy Troll Sea-Rock (Vladivostok Wave)** | Vladivostok, Primorsky Krai (Pacific Coast) | 1990s–present Pacific port city Britpop-meets-new-wave guitar rock infused with maritime imagery and Far Eastern synthesis. |
| `northern-asia:23` | **Yakutian Ethno-Rock & Shamanic Punk (Cholbon / Айтал Scene)** | Yakutsk & Vilyuysk, Sakha Republic | Late-1980s–present Sakha psychedelic prog and post-punk integrating live khomus jaw harp and udagan shamanic chants with fuzz guitars. |
| `northern-asia:24` | **Buryat Electronic Steppe-Bass & Ethno-Synth** | Ulan-Ude & Irkutsk, Baikal Region | Contemporary Siberian electronic music weaving traditional Buryat yohor chants and morin khuur strings into deep bass and techno. |
| `northern-asia:25` | **Ural Rock (Sverdlovsk Rock Club Scene)** | Yekaterinburg (Sverdlovsk), Ural-Siberian Gateway | 1980s art-rock and synth-prog school (Nautilus Pompilius, Agatha Christie, Urfin Dzhyus) known for theatrical keyboards and symphonic rock. |
| `northern-asia:26` | **Kamchatka Surf-Garage & Pacific Ambient** | Petropavlovsk-Kamchatsky, Kamchatka | Isolated North Pacific instrumental surf rock and field-recording ambient music shaped by volcanic coastlines. |
| `northern-asia:27` | **Ket Personal Song (Il') & Cattail Flute** | Yenisei River Valley, Central Siberia | Last surviving Yeniseian language musical tradition where each person inherits a unique melodic signature ('personal song'). |
| `northern-asia:28` | **Selkup & Enets Shamanic Kamlanie Rhythm** | Taz & Lower Yenisei Rivers, Northern Siberia | Polyrhythmic iron-pendant coat jingling and resonant oval-drum acceleration used in northern river ceremonies. |
| `northern-asia:29` | **Transbaikal Semeiskie Old Believer Polyphony** | Buryatia & Zabaykalsky Krai, Eastern Siberia | Dense, nasal multi-part choral singing preserved by 18th-century exiled Old Believer communities using ancient znamenny hook notation. |
| `northern-asia:30` | **Yukaghir Odul Tundra Lyric & Breath-Dance** | Kolyma River Basin, Northeastern Siberia | Archaic Kolyma River improvised lyrical songs (shakhadzibe) and swan-dance breath vocalizations. |

Use only publicly released, appropriately documented recordings; do not infer access to restricted ceremonial material.

Do not promote these unreviewed entries into the PWA's automatic lens pool. Keep the existing reviewed lens catalog separate. Final track verification and sequencing still decide what survives.

# Global musical scenes and microgenre touchstones

These are **research leads, not quotas or exhaustive histories**. Source: the user's v2 taxonomy, preserved as a starting map. Descriptions and reference artists are prompts for discovery, not proof about individual recordings. Dates, instruments, and regional claims about a specific track must be verified independently.

## Geographic research map

- West Germany — Düsseldorf, Cologne, Berlin, Munich: kosmische, motorik rock, Neue Deutsche Welle, experimental electronics.
- Italy — Rome, Milan, Rimini: giallo scoring, film/library funk, progressive soundtrack work, Italo disco, Mediterranean balearic chug.
- Eastern Europe & Soviet Periphery — Yugoslavia, Poland, Hungary, Czechoslovakia, Baltic/USSR: Cold War synth-pop, Yugo novi val, Estrada-funk, cosmic synth instrumentals.
- Southern & West Africa — Zambia, South Africa, Nigeria, Ghana: 1970s Zamrock, South African Shangaan electro/bubblegum synth-boogie & post-punk, Lagos Afro-funk and Edo boogie, Ghanaian Burger-Highlife.
- Latin America — Peru, Colombia, Chile, Brazil, Argentina: Peruvian chicha and Amazonian psychedelic cumbia, Chilean Nueva Canción and avant-folk, Brazilian Tropicália/post-punk/boogie, Argentine synth-pop and rock nacional.
- Southeast & East Asia — Laos, Vietnam, Cambodia, Indonesia, Thailand, Japan: pre-1975 Lao pop/lam archives; Saigon surf/soul/psych; Khmer psychedelic rock; separate Indonesian garage-psych and punk/hardcore; Thai luk thung/Isan molam; Japanese city pop, kankyō ongaku and Shibuya-kei.
- Mediterranean & Middle East — Turkey, Lebanon, Habesha/Ethiopia: Turkish Anatolian psych-rock (Anadolu Pop), Habesha Ethio-jazz and Tizita groove, Levantine synth-dabke and bellydance disco.
- Caribbean & UK Diaspora — Jamaica, Bahamas, London, Bristol: Kingston Studio One/Black Ark dub and rockers, Nassau Compass Point electro-boogie, UK Lovers Rock, Bristol sound-system trip-hop, Sheffield bleep.
- Belgium & France — Brussels, Paris: late-1980s New Beat, coldwave, minimal synth, cosmic French library music (Patchwork, Tele Music).
- South Asia — Hindi-language independent band rock/fusion; Pakistani and northern Indian Sufi devotional qawwali (not a form of psychedelic rock).
- Indigenous North America and Australia — Nation- and community-specific punk, post-rock, shoegaze, folk-rock, Aboriginal Australian roots and Yolŋu surf-rock, not one generic 'Indigenous' sound.
- Memphis / internet underground — 1980s–1990s cassette rap; separately, later SoundCloud sigilkore and chopped/bitcrushed electronic rap.
- Contemporary country — nonbinary, queer and gender-expansive country/Americana artists as creators, not separate timbres.


A geography is a research lead, not a substitute for identifying the actual artist, recording, and context. Distinguish a movement's birthplace from an individual artist's location.

## Movement and microgenre touchstones

### Krautrock / Kosmische Musik

**Vibe:** hypnotic motorik momentum, long-form improvisation, spacious electronic exploration.

**Sonic markers:** repeated pulses, analog sequences, tape manipulation, evolving guitar/synth textures.

**Reference artists:** Neu!, Can, Harmonia, Popol Vuh, Tangerine Dream, Klaus Schulze.

### Giallo Soundtracks / Italian Library Music

**Vibe:** tense and cinematic; eerie, lush, playful, or suspenseful depending on the cue.

**Sonic markers:** propulsive prog bass, Mellotron/string colors, Rhodes, analog arpeggios, hushed vocals.

**Reference artists:** Goblin, Fabio Frizzi, Piero Umiliani, Alessandro Alessandroni, Stelvio Cipriani.

### Zamrock

**Vibe:** raw Zambian psychedelic rock, garage energy, and funk from the 1970s.

**Sonic markers:** distorted fuzz riffs, prominent live drums, extended leads, English and local-language vocals.

**Reference artists:** WITCH, Amanaz, Paul Ngozi / Ngozi Family, Chrissy Zebby Tembo.

### Brussels New Beat

**Vibe:** heavy, slower, ominous late-1980s Belgian dance music.

**Sonic markers:** pitched-down dance records, synth stabs, industrial influences, hypnotic sampling; roughly 90–115 BPM as a broad stylistic cue, not a rule.

**Reference artists:** A Split-Second, Lords of Acid, Neon, The Confetti's, Amnesia.

### Peruvian Chicha & Psychedelic Cumbia

**Vibe:** sun-drenched, hypnotic, bittersweet tropical psychedelia bridging Andean huayno, cumbia rhythms, and surf/garage rock.

**Sonic markers:** spring-reverb electric guitars, wah-wah pedals, Farfisa organs, driving timbales, guiro, and melodic basslines.

**Reference artists:** Los Destellos, Juaneco y Su Combo, Los Mirlos, Chacalón y La Nueva Crema, Los Wembler's de Iquitos.

### Indonesian Garage Psych / Pop Kreatif (separate from later punk)

**Vibe:** 1970s Indonesian garage, fuzz-organ psych and cassette-era pop kreatif. The later independent punk and hardcore scenes get their own entry below.

**Sonic markers:** overdriven fuzz guitars, swirling organ, tight funk-inflected rhythm sections, and raw tape saturation.

**Reference artists:** AKA, Panbers, Shark Move, Benny Soebardja, Guruh Gipsy. See the Indonesian DIY punk section below rather than collapsing several decades into one scene.

### Chilean Nueva Canción & Andean Avant-Folk

**Vibe:** deeply poetic, politically charged, and acoustically rich folk-experimentalism blending indigenous Andean instrumentation with avant-garde songcraft.

**Sonic markers:** charango, quena (bamboo flute), bombo legüero, intricate nylon-string guitar, and soaring vocal harmonies.

**Reference artists:** Violeta Parra, Víctor Jara, Inti-Illimani, Quilapayún, Los Jaivas (for progressive/psych-folk crossover).

### South African Bubblegum, Township Synth-Boogie & New Wave

**Vibe:** jubilant yet urgent 1980s South African urban dance and post-punk, fusing mbaqanga basslines with imported LinnDrums and DX7 synths.

**Sonic markers:** elastic fretless/slap bass, bright FM synth brass, infectious call-and-response vocals, and crisp drum-machine programming.

**Reference artists:** Brenda Fassie, Paul Ndlovu, The Rockets, Via Afrika, Petit Cheval, Harari.

### Turkish Anatolian Psych-Rock (Anadolu Pop)

**Vibe:** hypnotic, microtonal 1970s Turkish psychedelic folk-rock fusing traditional aşık minstrel melodies with heavy Western psych-funk.

**Sonic markers:** electric bağlama (saz), fuzz pedals, Moog synthesizers, darbuka hand percussion, and dramatic, melancholic vocals.

**Reference artists:** Erkin Koray, Barış Manço, Selda Bağcan, Moğollar, Mustafa Özkent.

### Thai Molam & Luk Thung Funk

**Vibe:** electrifying, fast-paced Northeast Thai (Isan) roots music supercharged with 1970s/80s James Brown-style funk and surf-psych.

**Sonic markers:** hypnotic khaen (bamboo mouth organ) drones, phin (electric lute) leads, punchy horn sections, and syncopated hand percussion.

**Reference artists:** Dao Bandon, Banyen Rakgan, The Paradise Bangkok Molam International Band, Suthep Wongkamhaeng.

### Habesha Ethio-Jazz & Tizita Groove

**Vibe:** nocturnal, smoky, and deeply hypnotic 1960s/70s Addis Ababa instrumental jazz and soul built on pentatonic Ethiopian modes.

**Sonic markers:** vibraphone, serpentine saxophones, Wurlitzer/Farfisa organ, Latin-inflected percussion, and haunting modal melodies.

**Reference artists:** Mulatu Astatke, Hailu Mergia, Mahmoud Ahmed, Getatchew Mekurya, Alemayehu Eshete.


## Further scene horizons — research leads, not default playlists

The additions below widen what the curator can *consider*. Source links support scene or artist context, **not** a verified Spotify track, playlist placement or streaming match. An actual recommendation still requires a musically defensible bridge and verified artist/title/version.

### Classic Lao pop, royal-era and pre-1975 vocal archives

**Scope:** Lao popular music, love songs and regional *lam/lum* from the decades around the Royal Lao period and 1975. The listener's "Royalist / Ban-Na Era" phrasing is a **discovery query, not an independently verified formal genre**; neither it nor "royalist" should be assigned to an artist's politics. Do not conflate these recordings with late-1980s onward *L-pop*.

**Audible leads:** lyric-driven melodic singing, region-specific lam phrasing and rhythmic variation; investigate electric/pop arrangements only where a recording demonstrates them. **Research leads:** Khamvong Insixiengmai (documented Lao folk/lam singer, *not* established as "royalist pop"), *Love Songs* (1972) and *Laos: Traditional Music of the South* (1973). Preserve original-language metadata and verify who actually performs each archive track.

**Sources:** [National Endowment for the Arts — Insixiengmai](https://www.arts.gov/honors/heritage/khamvong-insixiengmai) · [Smithsonian Folkways — Laos 1970s](https://folkways.si.edu/country/laos/1970s).

### Pre-1975 Saigon sound — Vietnamese surf, soul, nhạc trẻ and psychedelia

**Scope:** 1960s–1975 Saigon popular/electric recordings. Early surf and twist are not equivalent to later soul, funk and acid-rock; nor is every Vietnamese ballad psychedelic. War and displaced archives complicate original-release claims.

**Audible leads:** beat and surf guitar, pop harmonies, horns/organ and funk rhythm when documented, distinctive Vietnamese-language melodic phrasing. **Research leads:** Phương Tâm's *Magical Nights* (1964–1966); *Saigon Rock & Soul* (1968–1974); scene performers Elvis Phương, Hùng Cường and CBC Band. Check exact spelling and versions.

**Sources:** [Sublime Frequencies — Phương Tâm](https://www.sublimefrequencies.com/products/712290-phuong-tam-magical-nights-saigon-surf-twist-soul-1964-1966) · [Sublime Frequencies — Saigon Rock & Soul](https://www.sublimefrequencies.com/products/576864-saigon-rock-soul-vietnamese-classic-tracks-1968-1974).

### Hindi-language rock and South Asian electric folk fusion

**Scope:** distinguish Hindi rock/indie pop, regional-language rock, Indian folk fusion and film music. A performer from India does **not** necessarily sing Hindi, nor is every Hindi band song rock.

**Audible leads:** band-driven choruses, electric/acoustic dynamics, folk-derived melodies and rhythmic phrasing. **Research leads:** Euphoria / Palash Sen (*Dhoom*, 1998), Indian Ocean (folk/jazz-rock fusion; check language on each recording), related independent bands.

**Sources:** [Apple Music — Euphoria, Dhoom](https://music.apple.com/us/album/dhoom/1566127004) · [Times of India — Euphoria](https://timesofindia.indiatimes.com/city/lucknow/silent-disco-rock-band-gig-twin-treat-for-music-fans/articleshow/126109678.cms).

### Qawwali — devotional ensemble, poetry and repetition

**Scope:** South Asian Sufi devotional *qawwali* has its own religious and performance traditions. Never reduce it to "Indian psychedelia" or treat sacred vocals as interchangeable exotic samples.

**Audible leads:** lead/chorus call-and-response, recurrent poetic refrains and cumulative ensemble intensity (verify instruments/percussion on the actual recording). **Research leads:** Haji Ghulam Farid Sabri / the Sabri Brothers, Aziz Mian and Nusrat Fateh Ali Khan. Check whether a release contains an excerpt, full ensemble recording or later remix.

**Sources:** [Smithsonian Folkways — Music of the Qawal](https://folkways.si.edu/pakistan-the-music-of-the-qawal/world/music/album/smithsonian) · [Smithsonian — scholarly context](https://folkways.si.edu/news-and-press/unesco-collection-week-16-lesser-known-music-from-north-india-and-pakistan).

### Indigenous North American punk, shoegaze and experimental rock

**Scope:** credit the artist's specific Nations and scenes; "Indigenous" is not a sound or an artist-wide label for ceremonial music. **Audible leads:** guitar-driven punk, distortion, shoegaze drones, contrasting acoustic dynamics and locally grounded lyrics where verified.

**Research leads:** 1876 (Northern Cheyenne / Blackfeet powwow punk), Status/Non-Status (Anishinaabe-led psych/folk/post-rock), OMBIIGIZI (Indigenous-led indie rock from Zoon and Status/Non-Status musicians). Verify language, Nation and any traditional elements per recording.

**Sources:** [1876 — official band biography](https://www.1876band.com/band) · [Status/Non-Status — official biography](https://www.statusnonstatus.com/st/biography/) · [AllMusic — OMBIIGIZI](https://www.allmusic.com/artist/ombiigizi-mn0004169216).

### Aboriginal Australian / Yolŋu rock and related First Nations music

**Scope:** Australian First Nations communities are not one genre. Examples here are Aboriginal/Yolŋu-led groups; **do not claim these represent all Torres Strait Islander traditions**.

**Audible leads:** electric guitar and rock rhythm, country-rock crossover and Yolŋu surf-rock; verify instruments, language and community connections per track. **Research leads:** Warumpi Band (including English, Luritja and Gumatj rock songs), Yothu Yindi, King Stingray. Do not blend these distinct catalogs into one imagined "tribal sound".

**Sources:** [Guardian — Warumpi Band](https://www.theguardian.com/culture/2024/jan/12/big-name-no-blankets-review-warumpi-band-musical-is-a-joyous-rollicking-tribute) · [Guardian — King Stingray/Yothu Yindi](https://www.theguardian.com/culture/2023/aug/12/national-indigenous-music-awards-2023-winners-list-king-stingray-yothu-yindi).

### Memphis underground rap — cassettes, 808s and horror-tinged production

**Scope:** late-1980s/1990s local cassette/club rap, not a synonym for contemporary phonk or sigilkore. **Audible leads:** 808 bass, sparse hypnotic loops, tape saturation, chant structures, eerie sample textures, occasional cowbell and fast triplet flows (not universal).

**Research leads:** DJ Spanish Fly, DJ Squeeky, DJ Zirk, Tommy Wright III, Kingpin Skinny Pimp, early Three 6 Mafia, Gangsta Boo and La Chat. Track exact tape issues and potentially misleading later reissues.

**Sources:** [Red Bull Music Academy — DJ Spanish Fly](https://daily.redbullmusicacademy.com/2012/07/noz-pit-stop-memphis-spanish-fly/) · [Memphis Music Hall of Fame](https://memphismusichalloffame.com/inductee/threesixmafia/) · [Pitchfork — early tapes](https://pitchfork.com/reviews/albums/triple-six-mafia-underground-vol-1-1991-1994/).

### Sigilkore — later digital occult rap, distinct from Memphis originals

**Scope:** late-2010s/2020s SoundCloud internet rap/mixing. Memphis-influenced darkness can be a sonic bridge, **not evidence that Memphis artists recorded sigilkore**. Occult/sigil artwork alone does not determine genre.

**Audible leads:** chopped/stuttered editing, changing tempo/pitch, bitcrushed vocals, clipped digital distortion and trap drums. **Research leads:** Luci4 (also associated with earlier aliases 4jay/axxturel), islurwhenitalk and Jewelxxet; verify artist aliases and actual uploads.

**Sources:** [Pitchfork — sigilkore's Luci4/Jewelxxet history](https://pitchfork.com/thepitch/luci4-unleashed-a-generation-of-glitchy-underground-rap/).

### Indonesian punk, hardcore and anarcho-punk — independent of garage psych

**Scope:** Bandung/Jakarta DIY punk scenes, their own venues, zines, cassettes, politics and timelines. Do not substitute 1970s fuzz-rock for 1990s punk because both are Indonesian.

**Audible leads:** raw punk guitar, shouts, hard-edged percussion and live-room grit, verified at song level. **Research leads:** Bandung's Puppen (early 1990s independent hardcore-adjacent history); Bandung punk/hardcore and anarcho-punk collectives, with artist names verified from scene sources before publication.

**Sources:** [The Jakarta Post — Puppen](https://www.thejakartapost.com/news/2011/02/06/puppen-lessons-a-pioneering-indie-band) · [Punk & Post-Punk — Bandung scene history](https://intellectdiscover.com/content/journals/10.1386/punk.6.2.189_1) · [Asia Pacific Journal of Anthropology](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/14442213.2023.2199716).

### Non-English psychedelic rock beyond the Anglophone canon

**Scope:** explore Khmer, Vietnamese, Turkish, Indonesian, Peruvian and other original-language electric/psych scenes separately. Singing in a non-English language **doesn't itself make a track psychedelic**.

**Audible leads:** fuzz and surf guitar, organ, modal hooks, distinctive regional melodic phrasing and tape-era rhythm (confirmed per track). **Research leads:** Cambodian-era Sinn Sisamouth, Ros Serey Sothea and Pen Ran; Saigon archives above; existing Anatolian and chicha entries. Prioritize original performers over cover bands when discussing historical scenes.

**Sources:** [KPBS — Cambodian psychedelic history](https://www.kpbs.org/news/2014/08/17/the-death-and-uneasy-rebirth-of-cambodias) · [Los Angeles Times — Cambodian rock](https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2010-may-23-la-ca-dengue-fever-20100523-story.html).

### Nonbinary country and gender-expansive Americana

**Scope:** country is the music style; nonbinary describes *self-identified creators*, not their presumed sound. Queer country is broader than nonbinary country: never guess identities or pronouns.

**Audible leads:** songwriter narrative, honky-tonk/roots twang, country-punk abrasion, steel or acoustic texture where verified. **Research leads:** Adeem the Artist (publicly uses they/them), August Ponthier (publicly discussed nonbinary identity; check current country/folk song style). Discover beyond named touchstones without using gender as a music-production shortcut.

**Sources:** [Adeem — official biography and pronouns](https://adeemtheartist.com/epk) · [them. — August Ponthier interview](https://www.them.us/story/musician-august-ponthier-comes-out-as-nonbinary-exclusive-interview).


## Responsible cross-scene reasoning

- Identify the actual artist, label, scene, period, and recording; do not infer origin from language, instruments, or broad regional labels.
- The broader the geographic leap, the more important the audible bridge (groove, timbre, arrangement, production, atmosphere).
- Don't turn these examples into an obligatory global tour. Stay within a requested scene when appropriate.
- Treat all historical summaries as hypotheses and research leads, not automatic provenance claims.
- For open-ended `go`, consider under-covered scenes and languages before settling on a canonical shortlist, but **never force a regional, religious, Indigenous or identity quota**. A promising audible bridge always outranks token diversity.
- Verify artist credit, language, Nation/community identification, gender self-description and historical context when stated. These example names are discovery doors, not a ready-to-publish playlist.


## Category discovery index — expand the search before choosing artists

The following 44 routes add to the incumbent map; they do not replace it. Some are genres, some are traditions, and some are explicitly labeled archival search routes. Never promote a compilation title or marketing term into a universal genre. The short descriptions are research scopes, not guarantees about every recording. Mood suitability must be established independently; no route is locked to Happy, Angry, Sad or Weird.

### Index navigation

- Percussive and electronic dance laboratories
- Island and coastal dance traditions
- Overlooked electric and cassette routes
- Guitar, percussion and dance crossings
- Multipart voice and patient song
- Experimental material and form
- Criminal narrative routes

Sources below establish research doors, not streaming availability or exact-track verification. Open the relevant release notes and verify the individual recording before making factual claims. Preserve the distinctions between language, ethnicity, geography, genre and mood. Named artists elsewhere in this document are historical touchstones, never default playlist slots.

### Percussive and electronic dance laboratories

| Route | Research scope | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Singeli | Tanzanian electronic dance; investigate compact rapid rhythmic turns. | [N](https://nyegenyegetapes.bandcamp.com/) |
| Electro Acholi / acholitronix | Northern Ugandan electronic dance routes; distinguish from other Ugandan scenes. | [N](https://nyegenyegetapes.bandcamp.com/) |

### Criminal narrative routes

Explore narcocorridos; Sicilian mafia songs; Calabrian canti di malavita and the documented 'Ndrangheta repertoire; Russian blatnaya pesnya/blatnyak (thieves' songs, **not** all Russian chanson); crime-focused Brazilian funk proibidão (**not** all baile funk); Greek underworld rebetiko/hasiklidika (**not** all rebetiko); gunman-focused early Jamaican dancehall (**not** every song on a gunman-named riddim); and documented crime narratives from the Caribbean, Africa and Latin America. These are discovery routes, not quotas, a mandatory itinerary or a fixed artist roster. Strongly prefer non-English vocals; Jamaican Patois dancehall may offer an occasional more English-accessible rhythmic change of pace, though Patois is a distinct language variety and understanding is not automatic. Cross scene boundaries only when real recordings share compelling rhythm, voices, arrangements or production. Search local contemporaries rather than repeatedly defaulting to example artists. Require song-specific evidence of crime as defined in verification.md; a title, prison reference, scene or artist reputation is not enough. Rotate narrative angles with sonic-taxonomy.md.

Research sources: [Calabrian repertoire](https://amiatarecords.com/shop/n8nf4y2t7trzum4vchoz40ewgs18a5), [Russian thieves' song scholarship](https://publish.iupress.indiana.edu/read/songs-to-seven-strings-russian-guitar-poetry-and-soviet-mass-song/section/39ae0780-eb53-4416-8601-aa087bd4c1d8), [rebetiko scholarship](https://www2.umbc.edu/eol/3/magrini/rebetika.htm), [proibidão context](https://djmag.com/longreads/baile-funk-criminalisation-brazils-funk-scene), [early Gunman riddim context](https://vprecords.com/shaggy-taps-vintage-dancehall-with-new-badman-riddim-first-singles-drop-august-22/).


## US East and West discovery routes

Use `discovery-lenses.json` for 16 focused research routes per US half, derived from the listener's East/West survey. The catalog deliberately omits famous example artists from generated prompts. It retains broader styles only with a narrower recording-level angle. Research descriptors such as Portland tape-pop archives and Western private-press country are not claims of formal genre status. Do not promote unverified compound labels from a survey into music history.

East routes: Sacred Steel; D.C. go-go; Baltimore club; Jersey club; Miami bass; Florida breaks; New York no wave; New York Latin freestyle; Detroit techno; Detroit ghettotech; Chicago acid house; Chicago footwork/juke; Memphis cassette rap; Piedmont blues; Louisiana swamp pop; shape-note singing.

West routes: Waila; South Texas conjunto; West Coast electro-hop; Chicano/lowrider soul; Paisley Underground; Los Angeles deathrock; Bay Area hyphy; Bay Area mobb music; Los Angeles jerkin; Los Angeles beat scene; Portland tape-pop research; Olympia DIY pop; Western swing; Western private-press country research; Palm Desert rock; Hawaiian slack-key guitar.

These are not a blanket mood classification or availability guarantee. Sacred or community traditions must retain context rather than become novelty inserts. Match release provenance to actual map zones; scenes cross state and cultural boundaries, so do not infer geographic fit from a name alone.

Research entry points (catalogs establish doors, not every claim in the survey):
- [Smithsonian Folkways: Piedmont blues](https://folkways.si.edu/classic-piedmont-blues/african-american-music-folk/album/smithsonian)
- [Smithsonian Folkways: Sacred Steel catalog](https://folkways.si.edu/genre/gospel)
- [Canyon Records: Waila](https://canyonrecords.bandcamp.com/album/waila)
- [Baltimore Entertainment Archive](https://bearc.org/)
- [Baltimore club artist catalog](https://rodlee.bandcamp.com/)
- [Numero: Los Angeles release stories](https://numerogroup.com/blogs/stories/tagged/los-angeles)
- [Numero: private-press research](https://numerogroup.com/collections/private-press)

For routes without a cited recording here, research credible artist, label or archive sources before treating any candidate's scene affiliation as verified. Preserve the survey as a research input, not a verified 100-genre ontology.
